## AI Research

### [Superintelligence this Christmas](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LJbKwctaioqp2Hi4b/superintelligence-this-christmas)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789689076/lexical_client_uploads/gt35nedsl0aitxgjfjwh.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it is plausible a strong form of recursive self-improvement</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref38gydbzed33"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn38gydbzed33">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is imminent or already underway, and that we may be on track for superintelligence by Christmas of this year if racing continues.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is substantially faster than any forecast, including ones like AI 2027 that were considered outrageously fast a year ago. It is faster than I myself expected even a week ago. I don't work at a scaling lab. I don't know more than is public knowledge. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let me be perfectly clear: what I am saying is absolutely nuts. Extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence. I claim we have now received said evidence and you should update accordingly.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> FOOM should probably should be your *default expectation*. </span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People have strong status quo bias. Your default expectation should be that things will radically speed up. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are not at the ceiling of intelligence. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should probably expect the transition to superintelligence to be incredibly fast. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffrrurd817r6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfrrurd817r6">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RSI is a positive feedback loop, so it is inherently (hyper)exponential. Everything is an S-curve eventually, but nothing suggests the ceiling is anywhere near human level, or that it happens at a human timescale. </span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI is capable of revolutionary advances in mathematics. Machine learning research is not different in kind. </span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Navier-Stokes was resolved with a counterexample, which is generically easier than a positive resolution. OpenAI has told the press it has substantial progress on a second Millennium Prize problem. Rumours name the</span><a href="https://x.com/dravenip/status/2098784606447169619" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Hodge conjecture for OpenAI </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[ and </span><a href="https://x.com/notjazii/status/2098475036684468297" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer for Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It is difficult to overstate just how incredibly hard these problems are. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Machine learning engineering is not substantially different from mathematics: fundamentally AI lives on computers. There is no or little 'real-world friction'. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot is made of progress being confined to domains with verifiable objectives. So the argument goes: AI can solve well-posed problems with a checker, and most of the world has no checker. There is some truth to this but it overstates the case.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These mathematical problems (Millenium prize problems, FrontierMath4) are well-defined but they have no clear gradient: you don't get 30% of a proof for 30% of the work. So this was long-horizon search without a dense reward. Regardless, progress in AI has overwhelmingly come from simple hillclimbing and picking low-hanging fruit. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effective long-horizon continual learning is likely the last remaining step to superintelligence. It could be a harder problem than a Millennium Prize problem; I consider that unlikely. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqdlwh8j0lhh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqdlwh8j0lhh">[3]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The speed of AI progress continues to be underestimated; by superforecasters and even by the researchers themselves. </span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Even after two years of enormous progress and a lot of updating ('feeling the AGI'), forecasts have still trailed what actually happened. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You Should Update On This. </span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Navier-Stokes and FrontierMath Tier 4 fell substantially ahead of every schedule I know of (numbers below). This should update you toward hyperexponential FOOM scenarios, not 'slow' takeoff. </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  Terry Tao in Nov 2024: FrontierMath will "resist AIs for several years at least".</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  </span><a href="https://x.com/ben_j_todd/status/2100240222290415916" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The typical AI researcher, Dec 2024: no AI solution of a Millennium Prize problem until 2054.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  FRI expert panel, Aug 2025: 55% on FrontierMath by end of 2027; 10% on an AI solving a Millennium Prize problem by 2027.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  Ben Todd, Aug 2025, bullish by his own account: 32% by end of 2026, 97% by 2030.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI models themselves, when asked, assign very low probability to a Millennium Prize problem being solved this year.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Actual: 40% at end of 2025, 94% in September 2026;</span><a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/noam-brown" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Navier Stokes Millenium prize problem settled last month, with rumors that two more are solved as well.  Even the developers of the technology itself are surprised by the speed - </span><a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/noam-brown" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Noam Brown, the RL lead of OpenAI </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">says he was taken aback at the pace of progress and hesitates to forecast beyond three months. </span></li></ul><p><br /></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal models are significantly ahead of released ones;  </span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intuitions from working directly with publicly available models is may be misleading about the pace of progress. The Navier-Stokes model, which started training on 28 August, is said to be "significantly more capable" than GPT-6 Astra. Anthropic has a model "somewhat more capable" than Mythos 5 it does not plan to release. Claude writes a large majority of the code merged into production.</span><a href="https://x.com/kliu128/status/2096616468851097811" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> OpenAI's own report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of 6 September  says its top 10% of researchers now spend $7,000 a day on tokens. That is about $</span><a href="https://x.com/MilesKWang/status/2096722337735516432" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2.5M a year per researcher, against a median new-hire salary of about $1.5M</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The median researcher spends $600 a day, up from $162 in July. Agent labour-hours passed human labour-hours inside OpenAI in June and stood at 3.14x by mid-August.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intuitions about timing from pre-training runs are misleading since most progress comes from RL, unhobbling and algorithmic innovations</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the previous paradigm the pre-trained models were already more than enough; unhobbling and RL was what was left.  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One such unhobbling is cooperating agent swarms. </span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Enter the Swarm </span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> OpenAI and Anthropic seem to have discovered how to use many instances of the same model ['agents'] together much more effectively. This massively multiplies the effective brainpower that can be brought to bear on one problem. 10,000 × 88 hours is 880,000 agent-hours.  Navier-Stokes was solved by a swarm of 10,000 agents. As a rule of the thumb firm productivity grows roughly with the square root of the number of employees so my guess is that this buys something like 100x over a single model.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic's own report states it has 30,000 agents running concurrently, and Claude has completely taken over 26% of all R&amp;D.  </span></h3><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789689134/lexical_client_uploads/qnmgwsbwspdrlxt4bwng.png" /><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789689971/lexical_client_uploads/xa6t02yxp493ohxw5tyx.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Linear extrapolation gives complete RSI summer of 2027. It's beginning to look a lot like FOOM.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn38gydbzed33"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref38gydbzed33">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are several interpretations of RSI. The weak version, AI doing most of the coding, is already happening. The strong version would be fundamentally new advances, not just scaling of previous approaches. Eg Strawberry/o1 &amp; reasoning models or Astra's neuralese. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfrrurd817r6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffrrurd817r6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In Hanson's model of growth modes [animal brains, humans, farming, industry] each mode grew about 100x faster than the one before, and each transition took a small fraction of the previous mode's doubling time. On his numbers the next mode doubles every week or two and the transition takes a few years at most. Note that while we have seen a somewhat continuous takeoff so far; that does not preclude FOOM.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqdlwh8j0lhh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqdlwh8j0lhh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Astra reasoning in pure neuralese, with no chain-of-thought, looks like a moderate performance improvement, slightly ahead of cautious trend extrapolation. That may understate it: neuralese is plausibly the key unblocker for effective continual learning.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LJbKwctaioqp2Hi4b/superintelligence-this-christmas#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Grantmakers aren’t afraid to die](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2o8B9hDN94k4Qine6/grantmakers-aren-t-afraid-to-die)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2><em>The AI Risk grantmakers do not act like they believe in imminent existential risk from AI</em></h2>
<p>The idea of "revealed preferences" is one of the most useful in economics; it allows us to cut through a great deal of metaphysical angst about what someone "really" believes, and focus on what they <strong>act like</strong> they believe, which is much more useful for making predictions about their future actions.  As one example, I grew up in a, shall we say, fervently-religious community, and it's often hard for nerdy Rationalist types to understand this, but: there are people who <strong>genuinely believe</strong> in Hell, and in Heaven.  They <strong>genuinely believe</strong> that moving souls from one to the other is the most important thing on Earth.  It's one thing to doubt the conviction of someone who lives an easy, staid, middle-class life... but for others, their choices and behaviors (e.g. years-long missionary trips) reveal their true preference and/or belief<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> beyond any reasonable doubt.</p>
<p>I bring this up because, per the actions and decisions of grantmakers operating in the AI Risk space, they mostly <strong>DO NOT</strong> seem to believe in imminent existential risk of AI.  On the contrary, they act like people who believe they are currently winning the game they're playing, and see no reason to change strategy.  It would be a shame if we went extinct because of that.</p>
<h2>The explore-exploit tradeoff</h2>
<p>One classic problem in decision theory is the "multi-armed bandit"<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span>, in which a player is faced with a bank of slot machines, each having an unknown payout probability. Each turn the player can pull the arm on a single slot machine and observe the result. Each result provides more knowledge about which machines have the highest probability of success, and so the player can choose each round which arm to pull, based upon what they've learned.  The player would like to optimize their overall winnings, and must balance <em>discover new information</em> (explore) against <em>continue current approach</em> (exploit).</p>
<p>This is a useful metaphor for life, and one obvious result is that the more confident you are that you know the best option, the more heavily you should lean into it. A corollary is that if your current best option isn't sustainable (e.g. in expectation you'll lose your money or your life), you should look harder for alternatives, because pulling "any unknown arm" is better than "an arm which probably loses".  In LessWrong-ese, this is like "playing to your outs"<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> in a losing scenario, by working towards possibilities that <em>could</em> win, even if they require more luck.</p>
<p>Working towards those possibilities is of course easier said than done... but "said and not done" is exactly what the grantmakers are doing!</p>
<h2>The evidence we're in "exploit" mode</h2>
<h3>Exhibit A</h3>
<p>Habryka recently acknowledged on Xitter that AI Risk grantmakers basically "rely on references from a very small inner circle"<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span>; see the discussion at:
<a href="https://x.com/BogdanIonutCir2/status/2091784358562590941">https://x.com/BogdanIonutCir2/status/2091784358562590941</a></p>
<p>That's the approach of someone who believes the game is going <em>just fine</em>, not the approach of someone who is trying to diversify for other options.</p>
<h3>Exhibit B</h3>
<p>A friend applied for a grant to study some aspect of compute verification<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span>, and was told "well, we don't have anyone who can evaluate this application, and we're not SURE that you'll do it right, because you don't have an XYZ expert as part of your team, so we're going to deny it".</p>
<p>That's not the approach of someone worried about losing <em>the game of human existence</em>.</p>
<h3>Exhibit C</h3>
<p>The whole comments section at:
<a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/B6d8Wzk4gNzHsXvdi/ai-safety-is-extremely-bottlenecked-on-grantmakers">https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/B6d8Wzk4gNzHsXvdi/ai-safety-is-extremely-bottlenecked-on-grantmakers</a></p>
<p>In particular, Scott Alexander says:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>I still can't figure out the world-model in which SFF exists and is net positive, CG is begging for more grantmakers, and all these organizations still aren't fully funded.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>To which Lukeprog's responses include:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>they could spend money in net-positive ways, but not above our ROI bar on the current margin</p>
<p>they acquired 'room for more funding' recently, but we funded them not too long ago and can't afford to investigate new RFMF claims for every grantee every few months</p>
<p>they're in our queue to investigate for renewal/expansion</p>
</blockquote>
<p>That's not the statement of someone who believes that the world is at risk!  If you think there's a good chance that the world ends soon, you spend the money that you have, while it's still worth something!</p>
<h2>Obvious verdict is obvious</h2>
<p>Do these actions seem like those of people who <strong>genuinely believe</strong> in the possibility of extinction risk due to AI, in the same way that a stereotypical fundamentalist Christian believes in Heaven and Hell?  I invite you to think about that for one minute, "by the clock".  I'll wait...</p>
<p>...</p>
<p>...</p>
<p>...</p>
<p>No, they do not.  Under that belief, these actions seem almost mind-bogglingly stupid, but I generally don't think of the EA/LW/AI community as being stupid.  Therefore, I conclude that they don't genuinely believe in near-term AI Risk, at least not on a deep, emotional level.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span>  I've seen the response about "gee, we have such a high bar", but Rationalists who <strong>genuinely believe</strong> in x-risk would make their BATNA "the human race goes extinct", not "we might hire a below-bar person".</p>
<p>In addition, those actions have the predictable side effect of opening up the community to zingers such as this<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-7">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span>, following the current METR kerfuffle:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>One of the aims of democratic society is to prevent you from needing to know the proclivities of particular individuals in the Bay Area. I would love it if we had a normal liberal-democratic process here, instead of one where I have to care about who is sharing funding, housing, and fluids in San Francisco and Berkeley.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Leaving such potshots open, in turn, undermines the credibility of the entire field.  I think METR is great, genuinely!  But if what we want is "lots of independent orgs also providing evaluations", then grantmakers would need to <strong>fund them</strong>, even if... no, <em>especially if</em> those people aren't personal friends.  There's no point in preaching to the choir.</p>
<p>Look, I get that sometimes there are a lot of factors to weight, and the Kelly criterion matters, and the math can be tedious.  If you need help with that part, call me!  But "extinction risk" and "short timelines" are going to strongly influence the answer, and given where y'all are starting from, it's directionally obvious what the result will be.</p>
<h2>Explore mode: Just do (good) things (better)</h2>
<p>As emotionally satisfying as it is to kvetch, I actually care about the outcomes here (remember: "rationality is systematized winning"<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-8">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-8">[8]</a></sup>
    </span>, and NotEveryoneDie is pretty worthwhile!), and so I'd rather focus on opportunities for improvement.</p>
<p>In industry, it's often the case that you might want a predictive model, but you aren't absolutely sure which variables are important; you also might be extremely concerned that building a model based upon your past successes could create a feedback loop that locks in your past biases.  Fortunately, there's a pretty robust solution: assign each candidate a score based on <strong>randomly-sampled</strong> feature-weighting, and then classify them (into e.g. "funded vs not") based upon that score (see e.g. Thompson sampling).  Then in the next round you re-calibrate your estimated feature weights based upon the predicted-vs-observed performance, improving your overall scoring algorithm slightly.  Rinse and repeat.  I propose that we apply this tactic towards evaluating grant proposals<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-9">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-9">[9]</a></sup>
    </span>.  And yes, I am saying that a <s>random number generator</s> <em>Thompson sampler</em> would quickly be a significant improvement over our current grantmakers.</p>
<p>Another tactic might be "stop waiting for our friends to apply for grants; instead make <em>new</em> friends by offering them money". We might apply this tactic by proactively offering funding<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-10">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-10">[10]</a></sup>
    </span> for graduate students and/or experiments to professors working on things like:</p>
<ul>
<li>game theory of verification and inspection games (did you know Krieger, who <em>literally wrote the book</em> on this, is still on staff at Juelich?)</li>
<li>cybersecurity of covert adversaries (Aumann is at Bar-Ilan University, and also just might have picked up a thing or two about game theory, on his daddy's knee)</li>
<li>actively paying for companies who produce network taps to create newer, more powerful ones.</li>
<li>etc</li>
</ul>
<p>If we're short of grantmakers and/or managers, then we might search for folks with some expertise in the field. Did you know that USAID was "fed to the woodchipper" not long ago? Many of those folks are experienced grantmakers!  Perhaps they could be helpful?</p>
<p>Not directly attributable to grantmakers, but another tactic might be "actively court people from all political persuasions, rather than e.g. rescinding their job offers because they're conservative"<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-11">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-11">[11]</a></sup>
    </span>.  Could be handy optics!  Grantmakers might, e.g. complain about those kinds of things, deliberately hold themselves at arm's length, seek out conservatives to be board members, etc.</p>
<p>Yet one more could be to provide automated, at-scale feedback to rejected applicants about what might have made them a successful applicant<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-12">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-12">[12]</a></sup>
    </span>.  There's lots of angst on things like the BlueDot Slack channel about "does anyone know how to have a good application for X?", and honestly it makes dating apps look positively friendly by comparison... at least those only take a few seconds to swipe, not hours of preparation time!</p>
<h2>Conclusion</h2>
<p>Our current grantmakers, instead of "trying a new slot machine" are spending funding rounds where they <strong>don't try any machines at all</strong>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-13">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-13">[13]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>They are playing as though humans are winning.</p>
<p>But we are not winning.</p>
<p>We are losing.</p>
<p>"Continuing to do the same thing, and expecting different results, is the definition of insanity", which, to be honest, doesn't sound very rational to me.</p>
<hr />
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-1"><p>For an EA-palatable discussion of what that belief can look like, see <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/your-book-review-a-residence-of-21">this book review</a> on Astral Codex Ten. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-2"><p>Herbert Robbins, <a href="https://projecteuclid.org/journals/bulletin-of-the-american-mathematical-society/volume-58/issue-5/Some-aspects-of-the-sequential-design-of-experiments/bams/1183517370.full">"Some aspects of the sequential design of experiments"</a>, <em>Bulletin of the AMS</em> 58(5):527-535, 1952, is the origin. For the modern treatment, Lattimore and Szepesvári, <a href="https://tor-lattimore.com/downloads/book/book.pdf"><em>Bandit Algorithms</em></a> (Cambridge, 2020), free online. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-3"><p>Jeffrey Ladish, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xF7gBJYsy6qenmmCS/don-t-die-with-dignity-instead-play-to-your-outs">"Don't die with dignity; instead play to your outs"</a>, LessWrong, April 2022. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-4"><p>He does say that an even better signal can be provided by writing ten blog posts, although I'm not sure he had one like <strong>this</strong> in mind (sorry, Oliver!).  Lest anyone think that I'm ranting because of sour grapes, I'll acknowledge that I've been working on this unfunded for several months, but have had an acceptable rate of acceptance/interviews/etc, so I'm not complaining about <em>personal</em> impacts, so much as <em>global</em> ones.  It is honestly a credit to the EA community that I can kvetch about this and think it probably doesn't hurt my odds. Much. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-5"><p>Which is literally the most important problem right now, IMHO; see my <a href="https://canaryinstitute.ai/blog/most-important-problem/">"The most important problem you've never heard of"</a>, Canary Institute, September 15, 2026. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-6"><p>And to be clear, I really appreciate the EA philosophy... I started donating to UNICEF for vitamin injections as a Starving Grad Student back in 2008, literally before the term "Effective Altruism" existed!  I write this essay because I think it might spur actual reflection, and possibly even change. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-7"><p>SE Gyges, <a href="https://www.verysane.ai/p/is-metr-a-meaningful-check-on-anthropic">"Is METR a meaningful check on Anthropic?"</a>, Very Sane, September 16, 2026. Responding to Dario Amodei, <a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier">"We must pace the frontier"</a>, September 2026, which names METR as the model embedded third-party evaluator. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-8"><p>Eliezer Yudkowsky, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4ARtkT3EYox3THYjF/rationality-is-systematized-winning">"Rationality is Systematized Winning"</a>, LessWrong, April 2009; the slogan "rationalists should win" goes back to his <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6ddcsdA2c2XpNpE5x/newcomb-s-problem-and-regret-of-rationality">"Newcomb's Problem and Regret of Rationality"</a> (2008). <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-8">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-9"><p>Note that "partial lotteries" are regularly run by mainstream science funders e.g. by "choose randomly above a quality threshold"; folks worried about x-risk could do that, too! <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-9">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-10"><p>See my earlier <a href="https://canaryinstitute.ai/blog/lessons-from-vannevar/">"Industrializing a small field: Lessons from Vannevar"</a>. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-10">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-11"><p>Richard Ngo, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FuGfR3jL3sw6r8kB4/richard-ngo-s-shortform">shortform entry describing a rescinded job offer</a>, LessWrong, August 3, 2026. Ngo reports Resolution (formerly Sequent) rescinded a Staff Research Scientist offer after learning of his publicly-expressed political opinions, with the concern being that other researchers wouldn't join if they saw his tweets. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-11">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-12"><p>Or at least, be honest with applicants about what you want.  Saying "you don't need AI safety experience, most of our successful fellow came from adjacent role" and then rejecting anyone without prior experience is... pretty messed up.  If you only want Harvard/Oxford grads, or 6+ years AI safety experience, then <strong>say so</strong>.  We might not HAVE six years for the newest crop of entrants to gain the experience they want... but in that case, maybe they could spend those years on careers they already enjoy, instead of scrambling for a new one? <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-12">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-13"><p>Julian Hazell, <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/AsxgoXZsuEEX6br6f/what-it-s-like-to-be-an-ai-safety-grantmaker-and-why-we-need">"What it's like to be an AI safety grantmaker (and why we need more of them)"</a>, EA Forum, March 2026: "We're leaving good grants on the table right now due to a lack of grantmakers." <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-CcxF7pwgJGSiCaXzC-13">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2o8B9hDN94k4Qine6/grantmakers-aren-t-afraid-to-die#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Pacing the Frontier: A Framework & Research Agenda](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E5SmpFsGPNpYjf92c/pacing-the-frontier-a-framework-and-research-agenda)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Below is the executive summary from our new paper at </span></i><a href="https://pacing.tech" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pacing.tech</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The full paper is available on the site and as a PDF. The full author list is Raymond Douglas, Charles Dillon, Nikola Moore, Gavin Leech, Shahar Avin, Mathias Kirk Bonde, Rohit Krishnan, Noah Perez, Nathan Young, Cormac Slade Byrd, Stephen Casper, Jan Kulveit, &amp; David Duvenaud</span></i><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Pacing AI” usually refers to how to conclusively handle the most extreme risks in the face of race dynamics. However, even for the goal of handling these highest-stakes cases, it's useful to take a broad view of pacing—one that encompasses all interventions aimed at moderating the pace of AI development, deployment, or diffusion. Thus:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Haphazard pacing is already common</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, including: delaying model releases for safety testing, pausing model development in response to shocks, and applying export controls.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current approaches will predictably fail.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Isolated, unilateral actions addressing only small fractions of the problem are not enough, but poorly executed interventions could easily backfire—good solutions will need to be carefully designed.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Precedents are being set whether we like it or not.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> How AI progress is paced now will shape how it is paced in future. We can learn from the shortcomings of existing attempts, and think about what precedents we are now setting for higher-stakes cases.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rapid, unpredictable progress requires an existing body of research to navigate well.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Pre-specified proposals aren’t enough when key decisions often depend on sensitive information, have to be made quickly, and are responses to surprises.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given all of this, we think it is time for pacing to be a dedicated research area.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though there have been many specific proposals, and though many subfields of AI risk largely exist to feed into pacing decisions, pacing as a whole has not yet cohered into a clear field of study. There are massive gaps in our understanding of pacing as a whole. Here we highlight three topics to illustrate the work that needs doing:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Understanding incentives.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The practical effects of an intervention will depend substantially on how the affected parties respond to them, including the actors who enforce the intervention. We recommend prioritizing research on:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How different forms of transparency about capabilities can help or hurt coordination;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to prevent mission creep among whoever is empowered to oversee pacing interventions;</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ways to align the incentives of different actors around pacing, e.g. by predictably compensating for losses with minimal moral hazard.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Improving technical and regulatory interventions.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> All interventions face tradeoffs, but new coordination and oversight mechanisms and prior planning can allow strictly better options. For example:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Designing regulatory structures that allow for rapid but limited interventions which can make room for more careful deliberation;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technical pathways to getting high assurance with minimal invasiveness, like LLM-based oversight and cryptographic guarantees;</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modeling the consequences of indirect interventions like buyouts, liability, and taxes.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Investigating the full lifecycle of a pacing intervention.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> By considering the full sequence from before to after, we can spot gaps. For example:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mapping different ways that interventions can end, and what this implies about different actors’ willingness to participate;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figuring out how to make interventions which are more robust to premature endings or imperfect execution, intentional or otherwise;</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dry runs and wargames to stress-test specific interventions.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For each topic, we include recommended reading and starting points in the full text. We invite readers to </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSeZCo85Uz9eIXjkPRl3F3lZJGObg4P3_4BgIoy40-m1EVBqhg/viewform?usp=publish-editor"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reach out</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> if they are interested in further work.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E5SmpFsGPNpYjf92c/pacing-the-frontier-a-framework-and-research-agenda#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [callcongress.ai – the basic action US residents can take to help with AI risk](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/C7Z5hr4yCG6fXy9o3/callcongress-ai-the-basic-action-us-residents-can-take-to)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm excited to introduce </span><a href="https://callcongress.ai" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">callcongress.ai</span></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">as a new site that makes it very easier to contact your representatives in Congress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Following recent events, people are updating about the extreme risks arising from AI development. Many have the natural and excellent instinct to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">want to do something</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you live in the US, then the basic action that pretty much anyone</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvnk19jh0q5i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvnk19jh0q5i">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can take is contacting their representatives in Congress and let them know that you are concerned and want action on AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A number of bills are in circulation right now that one can ask their representatives to support. Though even without mentioning specific legislation, I would guess it's still helpful to register general concern about AI and general directions that you'd like to see undertaken, e.g. pauses or slowdowns, transparency, talks and deals with China, etc.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 85.3125%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789606364/lexical_client_uploads/qmtqkp7givmqmr7le9tb.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">callcongress.ai aims to make the whole action convenient. </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Confirm or set your location (automatic detection is pretty good).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prepare your asks. The site lets your craft your own script but also provides a menu of positions and legislation you might want to use.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Use the provided phone numbers for your representatives to call them.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[optional] Pass along callcongress.ai to others.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The site also has a large section of quotes demonstrating the outpouring of support for concern about and action on AI. It's hard to take in just how far the Overton window has shifted, but I think it's never been easier to get people on board.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 91.08761329305136%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789617376/lexical_client_uploads/axftmcyzft91qrtlr8i5.png" /></figure><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Seeking feedback on policy</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A key part of callcongress.ai is its recommendations of legislation to support. I think it is a non-trivial question of which legislation ought to be support – not every bill about AI is a good idea. If you have opinions about which legislation is or isn't good, please let us know!</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">callcongress.ai is a project of Lightcone Infrastructure.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvnk19jh0q5i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvnk19jh0q5i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This includes noncitizens and people under 18 (</span><a href="https://www.brennancenter.org/sites/default/files/2020-10/AmicusBrief_house_%202020-08-31.pdf?inline=1" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">source</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/C7Z5hr4yCG6fXy9o3/callcongress-ai-the-basic-action-us-residents-can-take-to#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Did Galileo mistake Saturn's rings for Jupiter's Moons?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/h8qrA5t4LgZCiuEpK/did-galileo-mistake-saturn-s-rings-for-jupiter-s-moons)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tl;dr: No</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I intended to read </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1nMufGPE2jIGKyZBCh67LGt8wPxvgJ1zCXWh9jLrX9jg/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.ah97imo6k7ol" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Richard Ngo's Agency Curriculum</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> today. Unfortunately I didn't get more than halfway through the first reading of the first week of the curriculum.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reading is the blogpost </span><a href="https://jacoblagerros.wordpress.com/2017/10/26/the-copernican-revolution-from-the-inside/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">'The Copernican Revolution from the Inside</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">' by Jacob Lagerros. Broadly, it outlines the Copernican Revolution and explains all of its messiness. One of the things it argues is that, while correct (the earth does indeed orbit the sun), Galileo was overconfident and made many mistakes. So, on the subject of mistakes...</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lagerros' writes (talking of Gallileo):</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“And though he was also right about the existence of moons orbiting Jupiter, which contradicted the uniqueness of the earth as the only planet with a moon, what he actually observed rather seems to have been Saturn’s rings (Ladyman, 2001) [8].”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How could Galileo (an astronomer) get confused between Saturn and Jupiter? And if you look at Galileo's notebook sketches of Jupiter's moons (included in Lagerros' blogpost) then they clearly show 4 moons, changing their positions.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789646103/lexical_client_uploads/lfaj2rtlhfx9hs7w4sax.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How could someone who was observing Saturn's rings make sketches that look like this?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ladyman's book </span><a href="https://institutoscb.wordpress.com/wp-content/uploads/2020/03/ladyman-understanding-philosophy-of-science-routledge-2002-1.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Understanding Philosophy of Science</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is cited for this claim. The relevant quote from this book is as follows</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Although modern astronomers agree with Galileo about the moons of Jupiter, it now seems he mistook Saturn’s rings for moons, and also that, in some cases, he mistook optical illusions for craters on our own Moon. " </span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ie. Ladyman says that Galileo mistook Saturn’s rings for Saturn’s moons, not Saturn’s rings for Jupiter’s moons! </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I can't be bothered to check every claim made in the blogpost, but this is an error that is obvious and easily checked and conveniently seems to make the author's argument seem much stronger than it is so I'm going to treat the whole blogpost with a decent level of scepticism. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can look at Galileo’s notebooks containing his sketch of Jupiter’s moons above. These sketches clearly show distinct moons orbiting Jupiter, observed over a long period of time. It seems reasonable to conclude from this that he really had discovered moons orbiting a planet other than earth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Compare these to Galileo’s observations of Saturn from '</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_on_Sunspots" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Letters on Sunspots</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">':</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789646570/lexical_client_uploads/tnt3q7x4daakihfhayst.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or this from his notebook (</span><a href="https://science.gsfc.nasa.gov/attic/huygensgcms/Shistory.htm" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">source</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789646838/lexical_client_uploads/ibylqybjpckjown2osso.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/scan/manifest/1896PA......3..408P" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">other writings</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Galileo seems confused about his observations of Saturn and seems to understand that what he sees is not the same as what he observed with Jupiter. Presumably this is why, in his ‘Dialogue Concerning the Two Chief World Systems’ the argument mostly hinges on Jupiter’s moons (which again, he did actually observe) and doesn’t seem to mention the objects orbiting Saturn at all (maybe I'm wrong here, I just ctrl-F’d for 'Saturn' in </span><a href="http://www.gci.org.uk/Documents/Galileo_Galilei_Pythagorean.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this pdf</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I didn’t read the whole thing). Maybe Galileo was wrong about some other stuff, but his argument here (that he had observed bodies orbiting something other than the earth) seems reasonable to me!</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/h8qrA5t4LgZCiuEpK/did-galileo-mistake-saturn-s-rings-for-jupiter-s-moons#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI #186: The World Takes Notice](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aa3HprreFktzLQiaW/ai-186-the-world-takes-notice)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>In <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/jacob-coxon-warns-of-human-extinction?r=67wny"><strong>the wake of Jacob Coxon’s resignation</strong></a>, and the <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-extinction-risk-preference-cascade?r=67wny"><strong>resulting preference cascade</strong></a>, things have escalated quickly. The mainstream media picked it up.</p>
<p>Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei came out and said <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny"><strong>We Must Pace the Frontier</strong></a>, promising to take the unilateral first step of embedded investigators. OpenAI pledged to also take that step, and now both companies and Google are collaborating on safety.</p>
<p>The people took notice, raising both the salience that AI might kill everyone and roughly doubling people’s estimates of how likely that is to happen, from a mean of ~15% to ~30%. Many politicians called for regulations, guardrails and emergency hearings in Congress.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25619"></span>


</div>
<p>The most important thing became, and still is, to avoid political polarization. Through it all, I will keep reminding you to hold your fire, that attacks against Trump or against Republicans in general only make the situation worse, and that many Republicans, as I documented yesterday, are waking up and acting sensibly, including factions within the White House.</p>
<p>Alas, for now the wrong people, as in David Sacks, Mark Zuckerberg and Jensen Huang, have managed to convince Donald Trump to fully conflate existential risk with opposition to data centers, and Trump <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/trump-goes-full-hoax-on-ai-existential?r=67wny"><strong>has gone Full Hoax on AI existential risk</strong></a>.</p>
<p>There is also a systematic effort to launch hit jobs against all those who warn that AI might cause everyone to die, with the first concrete target being METR, and some rather extreme fire being focused on Effective Altruism, in ways that will reliably backfire but will not be pleasant for those who are in the crosshairs.</p>
<p>Also this week I covered two important things that happened previously: <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/brand-new-ai-solves-a-millennium?r=67wny"><strong>A new model solving a Millennium Prize</strong></a>, and Anthropic’s report on <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-bad-guy-with-an-ai-named-claude?r=67wny"><strong>various attempts to use Claude for malicious purposes</strong></a>, including fraudulent distillation attacks done at scale attempted by all the major Chinese AI labs, several of which also silently passed massive amounts of user data to Anthropic. Anthropic reports they are doing a remarkably good job containing all such threats, although of course we do not know that they have caught all the perpetrators.</p>
<p>I also finally got out <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/gpt-6-astra-can-do-ambitious-things?r=67wny"><strong>my capabilities review for Astra</strong></a>, which like Fable 5.1 is excellent.</p>
<p>This has been crunch time. Things will keep getting faster and weirder and scarier and more stressful, but I do expect that this last two weeks has been unusually fast and weird and scary and stressful. Hopefully we will get a relative lull soon, and I can get what passes for a break.</p>
<p>Currently in the post queue, always subject to change due to Breaking News, but this is what will happen if things are relatively quiet:</p>
<ol>
<li>Friday’s post will be The Preference Cascade is Only Getting Started.</li>
<li>Saturday’s post will look at Anthropic’s report on their alignment incidents.</li>
<li>Sunday’s post will talk about some issues regarding legal rules for AI loyalties.</li>
<li>Monday’s post would be the monthly roundup.</li>
</ol>
<p>I am very much hoping that is how things play out.</p>

<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/random_walker/status/2100176666664325223">Arvind Narayanan calls the growing AI spam problem ‘AI floods</a>,’ as in flooding communication channels, imposing time costs and often forcing them to shut down. The problem is the harms are diffuse and the situation is never an emergency, so we don’t do much about them, and then no one gets to send cold emails anymore.</p>

<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>


<p>Better late than never: OpenAI took 25% of its engineers, <a href="https://x.com/a16z/status/2099533700375662905">pointed Astra at its own systems and kept them there until they ran out of security issues</a>.</p>
<p>At least one instance of RSA-260 has been solved, up from a previous high of RSA-250, and one estimate says hyperscalers could currently probably factor RSA-1024 for on the order of $30 million a pop, less with optimization, which will presumably fall with time. RSA-2048 will take a bit longer.</p>


<h4>Astra Is Hard To Monitor</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.vanhollen.senate.gov/imo/media/doc/91026vanhollenastraopenailetter.pdf">Senator Chris Van Hollen sends OpenAI six pages of highly pointed questions</a> about Astra. He is understandably concerned about the lack of monitorability, and the lack of evidence of alignment. The letter got detailed enough I was half hoping a link to this blog would make it into the footnotes, which does reference Ryan Greenblatt. I especially appreciate asking what exactly is the limit in how much further monitorability loss OpenAI would accept.</p>
